On The Global Renormalization and Regularization of Several Complex Variable Zeta Functions by Computer
V.C. Bui, V. Hoang Ngoc Minh, Q. H. Ngo, V. Nguyen Dinh

Abstract
. This review concerns the resolution of a special case of Knizhnik-Zamolodchikov equations ( K ​ Z 3 KZ_{3} ) using our recent results on combinatorial aspects of zeta functions on several variables and software on noncommutative symbolic computations. In particular, we describe the actual solution of ( K ​ Z 3 ) (KZ_{3}) leading to the unique noncommutative series, Φ K ​ Z \Phi_{KZ} , so-called Drinfel’d associator (or Drinfel’d series). Non-trivial expressions for series with rational coefficients, satisfying the same properties with Φ K ​ Z \Phi_{KZ} , are also explicitly provided due to the algebraic structure and the singularity analysis of the polylogarithms and harmonic sums.

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{r}=\{(s_{1},\ldots,s_{r})\in{\mathbb{C}}^{r}|\forall m=1,..,r,\mathbf{Re}(s_{1})+\ldots+\mathbf{Re}(s_{m})>m\},

\displaystyle:=

For

\displaystyle\lim_{{z\rightarrow 1^{-}}}\mathrm{Li}_{s_{1},\ldots,s_{r}}(z)=\lim_{{n\rightarrow+\infty}}\mathrm{H}_{s_{1},\ldots,s_{r}}(n)=\zeta_{r}(s_{1},\ldots,s_{r})

\frac{dG(z)}{dz}=\Big(x_{0}\frac{dz}{z}+x_{1}\frac{dz}{1-z}\Big)G(z).

The series

is group-like

51
]
, meaning that there exists

such that

The challenge is then to determine

[
49
]

and to regularize

and

(although a lot of iterated integrals be divergent).
In

).

(1.11)

\displaystyle G_{0}(z)\sim_{0}z^{x_{0}}=e^{x_{0}\log(z)}

\displaystyle(\mbox{resp. }G_{1}(z)\sim_{1}(1-z)^{-x_{1}}=e^{-x_{1}\log(1-z)}).

After that, Lê and Murakami expressed, in particular, the divergent coefficients of

We will use also the isomorphy of the shuffle and quasi-shuffle bialgebras, to the respective enveloping algebras
of their primitive elements, leading to the constructions of dual bases to factorize characters thanks to the
Cartier-Quillen-Milnor-Moore and Poincaré-Birkhoff-Witt theorems (CQMM and PBW for short).

In Appendix, we will explain how (

,

The organization of this paper is as follows:

•

In Section
2
, algebraic combinatorial frameworks will be introduced.
In particular, we will give an explicit isomorphism,
\varphi_{\pi_{1}}
, from shuffle bialgebra to quasi-shuffle bialgebra
(Theorem
2.2
). Doing with
\varphi_{\pi_{1}}
, the construction by Mélançon-Reutenauer-Schüzenberger
(MRS, for short), initially elaborated in shuffle bialgebra and useful to factorize group-like series and then
noncommutative rational series (Theorem
2.7
and
2.15
), will be extended in quasi-shuffle bialgebra
for similar factorizations, via constructions of pairs of bases in duality.

•

In Section
3
, to study the structures via noncommutative generating series the polylogarithms and harmonic sums, at
integral multi-indices, will be encoded by words over various alphabets (Theorems
3.1
,
3.2
and
3.21
).

In particular, their noncommutative generating series will be put in the MRS form (their logarithms will also be provided in
Proposition
3.6
) and the bi-integro differential algebra of polylogarithms will be examined (Proposition
3.5
).

Concerning the polylogarithms at positive indexes, we will insist on the fact that their noncommutative generating series
is the actual solution of (
1.7
) and the noncommutative generating series of the finite parts of their singular
expansions corresponds to
\Phi_{KZ}
which will be also put in MRS form without divergent zeta values as local coordinates.

•

In Section
3.2
, with noncommutative generating series, the global renormalization and regularization of
polylogarithms and of harmonic sums will be achieved, providing Drindfel’d series (Theorems
3.7
,

3.16
and
3.29
).

With commutative ones, many functions (algebraic functions with singularities in
\{0,1,+\infty\}
)
forgotten in the straight algebra of polylogarithms, thanks to Theorem
2.15
, will be recovered.

On the other hand, since polylogarithms at negative indices are polynomial, of coefficients in
{\mathbb{Z}}
on
(1-z)^{-1}

(Propositions
3.14
and
3.19
) then the generating series of the finite parts of their singular expansions,
will precise the
regularization characters
(Corollary
3.10
and Proposition
3.24
) and will give examples
of Drindfel’d series with rational coefficients (Theorem
3.29
).

Combinatorial framework

In this section, coefficients belong to a commutative ring

6

6

although some of

.

A
and,

2.1.
Factorization in bialgebras

In section
1
, the encoding alphabet
X
was already introduced.
Let us note that there are one-to-one correspondences

(2.1)

\displaystyle(s_{1},\ldots,s_{r})\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}^{r}\leftrightarrow x_{0}^{{s_{1}}-1}x_{1}\ldots x_{0}^{{s_{r}}-1}x_{1}\in X^{*}x_{1}\displaystyle\mathop{\rightleftharpoons}_{\pi_{X}}^{\pi_{Y}}y_{s_{1}}\ldots y_{s_{r}}\in Y^{*},

where
Y:=\{y_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
and
\pi_{X}
is the
\mathop{\tt conc}
morphism, from
A\langle Y\rangle
to
A\langle X\rangle
, mapping

y_{k}
to
x_{0}^{k-1}x_{1}
. This morphism
\pi_{X}
admits an adjoint
\pi_{Y}
for the two standard scalar products
7
7

7

That is to say
(\forall p\in A\langle X\rangle)\;(\forall q\in A\langle Y\rangle)\;(\langle\pi_{Y}p|q\rangle_{Y}=\langle p|\pi_{X}q\rangle_{X})
.

which has a simple combinatorial description: the restriction of
\pi_{Y}
to the subalgebra
(A1_{X^{*}}\oplus A\langle Y\rangle x_{1},\mathop{\tt conc})
,
is an isomorphism given by
\pi_{Y}(x_{0}^{k-1}x_{1})=y_{k}
(and the kernel of the non-restricted
\pi_{Y}
is
A\langle X\rangle x_{0}
).
For all matters concerning finite (
X
and similar) or infinite (
Y
and similar) alphabets,
we will use a generic model noted
{\mathcal{X}}
in order to state their common combinatorial features.
Let us recall also that the coproduct
\Delta_{\mathop{\tt conc}}
is defined, for any
w\in{\mathcal{X}}^{*}
, as follows

(2.2)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathop{\tt conc}}w=\sum_{u,v\in{\mathcal{X}}^{*},uv=w}u\otimes v.

As an algebra the
A
-module
A\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle
is equipped with the associative unital
concatenation and the associative commutative and unital shuffle product. The latter being
defined, for any
x,y\in{\mathcal{X}}
and
u,v,w\in{\mathcal{X}}^{*}
, by the following recursion

(2.3)

\displaystyle w\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}1_{{\mathcal{X}}^{*}}=1_{{\mathcal{X}}^{*}}\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}w=w

and

\displaystyle xu\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}yv=x(u\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}yv)+y(xu\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}v)

or, equivalently, by its dual comultiplication (which is a morphism for concatenations, defined, for each letter
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
, by

(2.4)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}}x=1_{{\mathcal{X}}^{*}}\otimes x+x\otimes 1_{{\mathcal{X}}^{*}}.

Once
{\mathcal{X}}
has been totally ordered
8
8

8

For technical reasons, the orders
x_{0}<x_{1}

and
y_{1}>\ldots y_{n}>y_{n+1}>\ldots
are usual.
, the set of Lyndon words over

will

\mathcal{L}yn{\mathcal{X}}

is called the standard factorization
of a Lyndon
l
(and will be noted
(l_{1},l_{2})=st(l)

l=l_{1}l_{2}
and
l_{2}
is the longest nontrivial
proper right factor of

or, equivalently, its smallest such (for the lexicographic ordering, see

[
47
]
for proofs). According to a Radford’s theorem, the set of Lyndon words
form a pure transcendence basis of the
A

(A\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle,\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}},1_{{\mathcal{X}}^{*}})
.
It is well known that the enveloping algebra
\mathcal{U}({\mathcal{L}}ie_{A}\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle)

is isomorphic to the (connected, graded and co-commutative) bialgebra
9
9

9

A

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}}({\mathcal{X}})=(A\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle,\allowbreak\mathop{\tt conc},1_{{\mathcal{X}}^{*}},\Delta_{\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}},{\tt e})
(the counit being here

) and, via the pairing

(2.5)

\displaystyle A\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle\otimes_{A}A\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\longrightarrow A,

\displaystyle T\otimes P\longrightarrow\langle T|P\rangle:=\displaystyle\sum_{w\in{\mathcal{X}}^{*}}\langle T|w\rangle\langle P|w\rangle,

10

linear basis

Remark 2.3
.

In (
2.18
)–(
2.20
), if
A=K
, being a field, then
K\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle\otimes K\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle

embeds (injectively) in
K\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}^{*}\otimes{\mathcal{X}}^{*}\rangle\rangle\cong[K\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle]\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle
.
Indeed,
K\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle\otimes K\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle
contains the elements of the form

\sum_{i\in I}G_{i}\otimes D_{i}
, for some
I
finite and
(G_{i},D_{i})\in K\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle\times K\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle
.
But
\sum_{i\geq 0}u^{i}\otimes v^{i}
belongs to
K\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}^{*}\otimes{\mathcal{X}}^{*}\rangle\rangle

and does not belong to
K\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle\otimes K\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle
, for
u,v\in{\mathcal{X}}^{\geq 1}
.

Definition 2.4
.

Any series
S\in A\langle\langle Y\rangle\rangle
(resp.
A\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle
) is said to be

(1)

a
(resp.
\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}},\mathop{\tt conc}
)-character of
(A\langle X\rangle,\mathop{\tt conc},1_{X^{*}})
iff,
for any
u,v\in Y^{*}
(resp.
{\mathcal{X}}^{*}
), one has
\langle S|1_{Y^{*}}\rangle=1_{A}

(resp.
\langle S|1_{{\mathcal{X}}^{*}}\rangle=1_{A}
) and
\langle S|u\;\begin{picture}(220.0,580.0)\put(220.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(220.0,582.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{20.0}}\put(240.0,582.0){\line{ 0}{ 1}{10.0}}\put(230.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(225.0,587.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{10.0}}\end{picture}\;v\rangle=\langle S|u\rangle\langle S|v\rangle

(resp.
\langle S|u\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}v\rangle=\langle S|u\rangle\langle S|v\rangle,\langle S|uv\rangle=\langle S|u\rangle\langle S|v\rangle
).

(2)

an infinitesimal
(resp.
\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}},\mathop{\tt conc}
)-character of
(A\langle X\rangle,\mathop{\tt conc},1_{X^{*}})

iff, for any
u,v\in Y^{*}
(resp.
{\mathcal{X}}^{*}
), one has

\langle S|u\;\begin{picture}(220.0,580.0)\put(220.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(220.0,582.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{20.0}}\put(240.0,582.0){\line{ 0}{ 1}{10.0}}\put(230.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(225.0,587.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{10.0}}\end{picture}\;v\rangle=\langle S|u\rangle\langle v|1_{Y^{*}}\rangle+\langle u|1_{Y^{*}}\rangle\langle S|v\rangle

(resp.
\langle S|u\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}v\rangle=\langle S|u\rangle\langle v|1_{Y^{*}}\rangle+\langle u|1_{Y^{*}}\rangle\langle S|v\rangle,\langle S|uv\rangle=\langle S|u\rangle\langle v|1_{Y^{*}}\rangle\allowbreak+\langle u|1_{Y^{*}}\rangle\langle S|v\rangle
).
Moreover, if
\langle S|1_{Y^{*}}\rangle=1_{A}
(resp.
\langle S|1_{{\mathcal{X}}^{*}}\rangle=1_{A}
)
then
\langle S|u\;\begin{picture}(220.0,580.0)\put(220.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(220.0,582.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{20.0}}\put(240.0,582.0){\line{ 0}{ 1}{10.0}}\put(230.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(225.0,587.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{10.0}}\end{picture}\;v\rangle=0
(resp.
\langle S|u\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}v\rangle=0,\langle S|uv\rangle=0
).

Definition 2.5
.

For
(resp.
\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}
and
\mathop{\tt conc}
), a series
S\in A\langle\langle Y\rangle\rangle

(resp.
A\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle
) is said to be

(1)

a character of
A\langle Y\rangle
(resp.
A\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle
) if and only if

\langle S|1_{Y^{*}}\rangle=1_{A}
(resp.
\langle S|1_{{\mathcal{X}}^{*}}\rangle\allowbreak=1_{A}
)
and
\langle S|u\;\begin{picture}(220.0,580.0)\put(220.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(220.0,582.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{20.0}}\put(240.0,582.0){\line{ 0}{ 1}{10.0}}\put(230.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(225.0,587.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{10.0}}\end{picture}\;v\rangle=\langle S|u\rangle\langle S|v\rangle

(resp.
\langle S|u\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}v\rangle=\langle S|u\rangle\langle S|v\rangle
and
\langle S|uv\rangle=\langle S|u\rangle\langle S|v\rangle
).

(2)

an infinitesimal character of
A\langle Y\rangle
(resp.
A\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle
) if and only if

\langle S|u\;\begin{picture}(220.0,580.0)\put(220.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(220.0,582.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{20.0}}\put(240.0,582.0){\line{ 0}{ 1}{10.0}}\put(230.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(225.0,587.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{10.0}}\end{picture}\;v\rangle=\langle S|u\rangle\langle v|1_{Y^{*}}\rangle+\langle u|1_{Y^{*}}\rangle\langle S|v\rangle

(resp.
\langle S|u\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}v\rangle=\langle S|u\rangle\langle v|1_{Y^{*}}\rangle+\langle u|1_{Y^{*}}\rangle\langle S|v\rangle

and
\langle S|uv\rangle=\langle S|u\rangle\langle v|1_{Y^{*}}\rangle+\langle u|1_{Y^{*}}\rangle\langle S|v\rangle
),
for
u
and
v\in Y^{*}
(resp.
{\mathcal{X}}^{*}
).
Moreover, if
\langle S|1_{Y^{*}}\rangle=1_{A}
(resp.
\langle S|1_{{\mathcal{X}}^{*}}\rangle=1_{A}
) then

\langle S|u\;\begin{picture}(220.0,580.0)\put(220.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(220.0,582.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{20.0}}\put(240.0,582.0){\line{ 0}{ 1}{10.0}}\put(230.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(225.0,587.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{10.0}}\end{picture}\;v\rangle=0
(resp.
\langle S|u\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}v\rangle=0
and
\langle S|uv\rangle=0
).

Definition 2.6
.

Let
S\in A\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle
(resp.
A\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle
) and
P\in A\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle
(resp.
A\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle
).

(1)

The
left
(resp.
right
)
shift
14
14

14

These are called
residuals

and extend shifts of functions in harmonic analysis
[

]
.

of
S
by
P
, is
P\triangleright S
(resp.
S\triangleleft P
) defined, for any
w\in{\mathcal{X}}^{*}
, by

\langle{P\triangleright S}|w\rangle=\langle S|wP\rangle
(resp.
\langle{S\triangleleft P}|w\rangle=\langle S|Pw\rangle
).

(2)

Let
S\in A\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle
such that
\langle S|1_{{\mathcal{X}}^{*}}\rangle=0
.
The Kleene star of
S
, denoted by
S^{*}
, is the infinite sum
1+S+S^{2}+\cdots
.

(3)

Let
A=K
be a field. One defines also the Sweedler’s dual

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}}^{\circ}({\mathcal{X}})
(resp.
{\mathcal{H}}_{\;\begin{picture}(220.0,580.0)\put(220.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(220.0,582.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{20.0}}\put(240.0,582.0){\line{ 0}{ 1}{10.0}}\put(230.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(225.0,587.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{10.0}}\end{picture}\;}^{\circ}(Y)
) of

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}}({\mathcal{X}})
(resp.
{\mathcal{H}}_{\;\begin{picture}(220.0,580.0)\put(220.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(220.0,582.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{20.0}}\put(240.0,582.0){\line{ 0}{ 1}{10.0}}\put(230.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(225.0,587.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{10.0}}\end{picture}\;}(Y)
), for a finite set
I
by

\displaystyle S\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}}^{\circ}({\mathcal{X}})\mbox{ (resp. ${\mathcal{H}}_{\;\begin{picture}(220.0,580.0)\put(220.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(220.0,582.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{20.0}}\put(240.0,582.0){\line{ 0}{ 1}{10.0}}\put(230.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(225.0,587.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{10.0}}\end{picture}\;}^{\circ}(Y)$)}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathop{\tt conc}}(S)=\sum_{i\in I}G_{i}\otimes D_{i},

where
\{G_{i},D_{i}\}_{i\in I}
are series which can be chosen in
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}}^{\circ}({\mathcal{X}})
(see
[

]
).

Theorem 2.7

(
[
19
,

,

,

]
)
.

Let
S\in A\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle
. Then the following assertions are equivalent

(1)

\{S\triangleleft w\}_{w\in{\mathcal{X}}^{*}}
(resp.
\{w\triangleright S\}_{w\in{\mathcal{X}}^{*}}
)
lie in a finitely generated shift-invariant
A
-module
[

]
.

(2)

The series
S
belongs to the (algebraic) closure of
\widehat{A.{\mathcal{X}}}
by the rational operations
15
15

15

In here,
\widehat{A.{\mathcal{X}}}
is understood as the set of all homogenuous series of degree
1
, of the form

\sum\limits_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}a_{x}x
.

\{\mathop{\tt conc},+,*\}
(within
A\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle
).

(3)

There is a linear representation
(\nu,\mu,\eta)
, of rank
n
, for
S
with

\nu\in M_{1,n}(A),\allowbreak\eta\in M_{n,1}(A)
and
\mu:{\mathcal{X}}^{*}\rightarrow M_{n,n}(A)

such that
\langle S|w\rangle=\nu\mu(w)\eta
, for
w\in{\mathcal{X}}^{*}
.

A series satisfying one of the conditions of Theorem
2.7
is called
rational
.
The set of these series, a
A
-module
16
16

16

In fact (we will see it) a unital
A
-algebra for

\mathop{\tt conc}
and
\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}.
denoted by
{A^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle}
, isclosed by
\{\mathop{\tt conc},+,*\}
.
The set of rational series,
a
A
-module, is denoted by
{A^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle}
(which is closed by
\{\mathop{\tt conc},+,*\}
).

Remark 2.8
.

With the notations in Definition
2.6
, these shifts are associative and mutually commute,
i.e.

\displaystyle S\triangleleft(PR)=(S\triangleleft P)\triangleleft R,

\displaystyle P\triangleright(R\triangleright S)=(PR)\triangleright S,

\displaystyle(P\triangleleft S)\triangleright R=P\triangleleft(S\triangleright R)

and then

\displaystyle\forall x,y\in{\mathcal{X}},

\displaystyle\forall w\in{\mathcal{X}}^{*},

\displaystyle x\triangleright(wy)=(yw)\triangleleft x=\delta_{x}^{y}w.

Corollary 2.9

(
[
41
]
)
.

With notations in Definition
2.5
, if
A
is a field
K
then there exists a finite double family of series

(G_{i},D_{i})_{i\in I}
which satisfies three assertions of Theorem
2.6
or, equivalently, one of the following assertions holds.

4.

\langle S|PQ\rangle=\sum\limits_{i\in I}\langle G_{i}|P\rangle\langle D_{i}|Q\rangle
, for
P
and
Q\in K\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle
(resp.
\in K\langle Y\rangle
),

5.

\Delta_{\mathop{\tt conc}}(S)=\sum\limits_{i\in I}G_{i}\otimes D_{i}
.

Hence, the Sweedler’s dual of the bialgebra
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}}({\mathcal{X}})
(resp.
{\mathcal{H}}_{\;\begin{picture}(220.0,580.0)\put(220.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(220.0,582.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{20.0}}\put(240.0,582.0){\line{ 0}{ 1}{10.0}}\put(230.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(225.0,587.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{10.0}}\end{picture}\;}(Y)
) is isomorphic to

(K^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle,\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}},1_{{\mathcal{X}}^{*}},\Delta_{\mathop{\tt conc}})
(resp.
(K^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle Y\rangle\rangle,\;\begin{picture}(220.0,580.0)\put(220.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(220.0,582.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{20.0}}\put(240.0,582.0){\line{ 0}{ 1}{10.0}}\put(230.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(225.0,587.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{10.0}}\end{picture}\;,1_{Y^{*}},\Delta_{\mathop{\tt conc}}
).

Proof.

Let
(\beta,\mu,\eta)
of dimension
n
be a linear representation of
S\in K^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle
.
It can be also associated to the following linear representations of dimension
n

\displaystyle\forall 1\leq i\leq n,\quad(\beta,\mu,e_{i})\mbox{ of }L_{i}

and

\displaystyle({}^{t}e_{i},\mu,\eta)\mbox{ of }R_{i},

\displaystyle\mbox{where }e_{i}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{1,n}(K)

and

\displaystyle{}^{t}e_{i}=\begin{matrix}(0&\ldots&0&1\!\!_{{}_{{}_{\displaystyle i}}}&0&\ldots&0)\end{matrix}.

Hence, using the morphism of monoids
\mu
, one has, for any
u
and
v\in{\mathcal{X}}^{*}
,

\displaystyle\langle S|uv\rangle=\beta\mu(u)\mu(v)\eta=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\beta\mu(u)e_{i})({}^{t}e_{i}\mu(v)\eta)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle L_{i}|u\rangle\langle R_{i}|v\rangle,

\displaystyle\langle\Delta_{\tt conc}(S)|u\otimes v\rangle=\langle S|uv\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle L_{i}|u\rangle\langle R_{i}|v\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle L_{i}\otimes R_{i}|u\otimes v\rangle.

One deduces then the following criterion yielding the expected results

\displaystyle S\in K^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle\iff\Delta_{\tt conc}(S)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}\otimes R_{i}.

∎

One also has the following constructions of linear representations (only the last one is new and
the first ones are already treated in

]
, see also

R_{1}\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}R_{2}

base on coproducts and tensor products of linear representations.

Proposition 2.10
.

The module
{A^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle}
(resp.
{A^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle Y\rangle\rangle}
)
is closed by
\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}
(resp.
). Moreover, for any
i=1,2
, let

R_{i}\in{A^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle}
and
(\nu_{i},\mu_{i},\eta_{i})
be its
representation of dimension
n_{i}
. Then the linear representation of

\displaystyle R_{i}^{*}

is

\displaystyle\Big(\begin{pmatrix}0&1\end{pmatrix},\left\{\begin{pmatrix}\mu_{i}(x)+\eta_{i}\nu_{i}\mu_{i}(x)&0\cr\nu_{i}\eta_{i}&0\end{pmatrix}\right\}_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}},\begin{pmatrix}\eta_{i}\cr 1\end{pmatrix}\Big),

\displaystyle\mbox{that of }R_{1}+R_{2}

is

\displaystyle\Big(\begin{pmatrix}\nu_{1}&\nu_{2}\end{pmatrix},\left\{\begin{pmatrix}\mu_{1}(x)&{\bf 0}\cr{\bf 0}&\mu_{2}(x)\end{pmatrix}\right\}_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}},\begin{pmatrix}\eta_{1}\cr\eta_{2}\end{pmatrix}\Big),

\displaystyle\mbox{that of }R_{1}R_{2}

is

\displaystyle\Big(\begin{pmatrix}\nu_{1}&0\end{pmatrix},\left\{\begin{pmatrix}\mu_{1}(x)&\eta_{1}\nu_{2}\mu_{2}(x)\cr 0&\mu_{2}(x)\end{pmatrix}\right\}_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}},\begin{pmatrix}\eta_{1}\mu_{2}\eta_{2}\cr\eta_{2}\end{pmatrix}\Big),

\displaystyle\mbox{that of }R_{1}\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}R_{2}

is

\displaystyle(\nu_{1}\otimes\nu_{2},\{\mu_{1}(x)\otimes\mathrm{I}_{n_{2}}+\mathrm{I}_{n_{1}}\otimes\mu_{2}(x)\}_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}},\eta_{1}\otimes\eta_{2}),

\displaystyle\mbox{that of }R_{1}\;\begin{picture}(220.0,580.0)\put(220.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(220.0,582.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{20.0}}\put(240.0,582.0){\line{ 0}{ 1}{10.0}}\put(230.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(225.0,587.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{10.0}}\end{picture}\;R_{2}

is

\displaystyle(\nu_{1}\otimes\nu_{2},\{\mu_{1}(y_{k})\otimes\mathrm{I}_{n_{2}}+\mathrm{I}_{n_{1}}\otimes\mu_{2}(y_{k})

\displaystyle+\sum_{i+j=k}\mu_{1}(y_{i})\otimes\mu_{2}(y_{j})\}_{k\geq 1},\eta_{1}\otimes\eta_{2}).

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\cr(-t^{2}x_{0}x_{1})^{*}\leftrightarrow(\nu_{2},\{\mu_{2}(x_{0}),\mu_{2}(x_{1})\},\eta_{2}),&&(t^{2}x_{0}x_{1})^{*}\leftrightarrow(\nu_{1},\{\mu_{1}(x_{0}),\mu_{1}(x_{1})\},\eta_{1}).\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{rlrl}\nu_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\end{pmatrix},&\mu_{1}(x_{0})=\begin{pmatrix}0&t\cr 0&0\end{pmatrix},&\mu_{1}(x_{1})=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\cr t&0\end{pmatrix},&\eta_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}1\cr 0\end{pmatrix},\cr\nu_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\end{pmatrix},&\mu_{2}(x_{0})=\begin{pmatrix}0&\mathrm{i}t\cr 0&0\end{pmatrix},&\mu_{2}(x_{1})=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\cr\mathrm{i}t&0\end{pmatrix},&\eta_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}1\cr 0\end{pmatrix}.\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{rcl}(-t^{2}x_{0}x_{1})^{*}\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}(t^{2}x_{0}x_{1})^{*}&\leftrightarrow&(\nu,\{\mu(x_{0}),\mu(x_{1})\},\eta)\\
&=&(\nu_{1}\otimes\nu_{2},\{\mu_{1}(x_{0})\otimes\mathrm{I}_{n_{2}}+\mathrm{I}_{n_{1}}\otimes\mu_{2}(x_{0}),\\
&&\mu_{1}(x_{1})\otimes\mathrm{I}_{n_{2}}+\mathrm{I}_{n_{1}}\otimes\mu_{2}(x_{1}),\eta_{1}\otimes\eta_{2}).\end{array}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\cr\begin{array}[]{r}(-t^{2}y_{2})^{*}\leftrightarrow(\nu_{2},\mu_{2}(y_{2}),\eta_{2})\\
=(1,-t^{2},1),\end{array}\begin{array}[]{r}(t^{2}y_{2})^{*}\leftrightarrow(\nu_{1},\mu_{1}(y_{2}),\eta_{1})\\
=(1,t^{2},1),\end{array}\begin{array}[]{r}(-t^{4}y_{4})^{*}\leftrightarrow(\nu,\mu(y_{4}),\eta)\\
=(1,-t^{4},1).\end{array}\end{array}

Definition 2.13
.

Any series
S\in A\langle\langle{{\mathcal{X}}}\rangle\rangle
is called

(1)

syntactically exchangeable if and only if it is constant on multi-homogeneous classes,
i.e.

\begin{array}[]{lcr}(\forall u,v\in{\mathcal{X}}^{*})([(\forall x\in{\mathcal{X}})(|u|_{x}=|v|_{x})]&\Rightarrow&\langle S|u\rangle=\langle S|v\rangle).\end{array}

The set of syntactically exchangeable series is denoted by
A_{\mathrm{exc}}^{\mathrm{synt}}\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle
.

(2)

rationally exchangeable if and only if it admits a representation
(\nu,\mu,\eta)

such that the matrices
\{\mu(x)\}_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}
commute and the set of these series, a shuffle
subalgebra of
A\langle\langle X\rangle\rangle
, is denoted by
A_{\mathrm{exc}}^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle
.

Remark 2.14
.

S
is syntactically exchangeable if and only if it is of the form

\displaystyle S=\sum_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{({\mathcal{X}})},\mathrm{supp}(\alpha)=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\}}s_{\alpha},x_{1}^{\alpha(x_{1})}\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}\ldots\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}x_{k}^{\alpha(x_{k})}.

When
A
is a field, the rational exchangeable series are exactly those who admit a representation with commuting matrices
(at least the minimal one is such) and it is taken as definition as, even for rings, implying syntactic exchangeability.

Theorem 2.15

(See
[
25
,
41
]
)
.

(1)

In all cases, one has

\displaystyle A_{\mathrm{exc}}^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle\subset A^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle\cap A_{\mathrm{exc}}^{\mathrm{synt}}\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle.

The equality holds when
A
is a field and

\displaystyle A_{\mathrm{exc}}^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle X\rangle\rangle=A^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle x_{0}\rangle\rangle\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}A^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle x_{1}\rangle\rangle=\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}\limits_{x\in X}A^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle x\rangle\rangle,

\displaystyle A_{\mathrm{exc}}^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle Y\rangle\rangle\cap A_{\mathrm{fin}}^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle Y\rangle\rangle=\bigcup\limits_{k\geq 0}\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}\limits_{j=1}^{k}A^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle y_{j}\rangle\rangle\subsetneq A_{\mathrm{exc}}^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle Y\rangle\rangle,

where

A_{\mathrm{fin}}^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle Y\rangle\rangle=\bigcup\limits_{F\subset_{finite}Y}A^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle F\rangle\rangle
,
the algebra of series over finite subalphabets.

(2)

(Kronecker’s theorem
[
1
]
) One has

A^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle x\rangle\rangle=\{P(1-xQ)^{-1}\}_{P,Q\in A[x]}
(for
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
) and if
A=K

then it is an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero one also has

K^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle x\rangle\rangle=\mathop{\mathrm{}}{span}\nolimits_{K}\{(ax)^{*}\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}K\langle x\rangle|a\in K\}
.

(3)

Series
\Big(\sum\limits_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\alpha_{x}x\Big)^{*}
are
\mathop{\tt conc}
-characters. Any

\mathop{\tt conc}
-character is of this form.

(4)

A
is supposed without zero divisors. If the family
(\varphi_{i})_{i\in I}
is
{\mathbb{Z}}
-linearly independent within
\widehat{A{\mathcal{X}}}
then the
family
\mathcal{L}yn({\mathcal{X}})\uplus\{\varphi_{i}^{*}\}_{i\in I}
is
A
-algebraically free within
(A^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle,\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}},1_{{\mathcal{X}}^{*}})
.

(5)

In particular, if
A
is a ring without zero divisors

\{x^{*}\}_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}
(resp.
\{y^{*}\}_{y\in Y}
) are algebraically independent over

(A\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle,\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}},1_{{\mathcal{X}}^{*}})
(resp.
(A\langle Y\rangle,\;\begin{picture}(220.0,580.0)\put(220.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(220.0,582.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{20.0}}\put(240.0,582.0){\line{ 0}{ 1}{10.0}}\put(230.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(225.0,587.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{10.0}}\end{picture}\;,1_{Y^{*}})
)
within
(A^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle,\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}},1_{{\mathcal{X}}^{*}})

(resp.
(A^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle Y\rangle\rangle,\;\begin{picture}(220.0,580.0)\put(220.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(220.0,582.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{20.0}}\put(240.0,582.0){\line{ 0}{ 1}{10.0}}\put(230.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(225.0,587.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{10.0}}\end{picture}\;,1_{Y^{*}})
).

Proof.

(1)

The inclusion is obvious in view of Definition
2.13
.
For the equality, it suffices to prove that, when
A
is a field, every rational and
exchangeable series admits a representation with commuting matrices. This is true of
any minimal representation as shows the computation of shifts (see
[
19
,
25
,
41
]
).

Now, if
{\mathcal{X}}
is finite, then (all matrices commute)

\displaystyle\sum_{w\in{\mathcal{X}}^{*}}\mu(w)w=\Big(\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\mu(x)x\Big)^{*}=\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}(\mu(x)x)^{*}

and the result comes from the fact that
R
is a linear combination of matrix elements.
As regards the second equality, inclusion
\supset
is straightforward. We remark that

\bigcup\limits_{k\geq 1}A^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle y_{1}\rangle\rangle\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}\ldots\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}A^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle y_{k}\rangle\rangle

is directed as these algebras are nested in one another. With this in view, the reverse inclusion comes from the fact that every

S\in A_{\mathrm{fin}}^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle Y\rangle\rangle
is a series over a finite alphabet and the result follows from the first equality.

(2)

Let
\mathcal{A}=\{P(1-xQ)^{-1}\}_{P,Q\in A[x]}
. Since
P(1-xQ)^{-1}=P(xQ)^{*}
then it is obvious that

\mathcal{A}\subset A^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle x\rangle\rangle
. Next, it is easy to check that
\mathcal{A}
contains
A\langle x\rangle(=A[x])

and is closed by
+
and
\mathop{\tt conc}
as, for instance,

\displaystyle(1-xQ_{1})(1-xQ_{2})=(1-x(Q_{1}+Q_{2}-xQ_{1}Q_{2})).

We need to prove that
\mathcal{A}
is closed for
*
. For this to be applied to
P(1-xQ)^{-1}
,
we must suppose that
P(0)=0
(as, indeed,
\langle P(1-xQ)^{-1}|1_{x^{*}}\rangle=P(0)
) and,
in this case,
P=xP_{1}
. Now

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{P}{1-xQ}\Big)^{*}=\Big(1-\frac{P}{1-xQ}\Big)^{-1}=\frac{1-xQ}{1-x(Q+P_{1})}\in\mathcal{A}.

(3)

Let
S=\Big(\sum\limits_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\alpha_{x}\,x\Big)^{*}
. Then
\langle S|1_{{\mathcal{X}}^{*}}\rangle=1_{A}
. Furthermore, if
w=xu
then
\langle S|xu\rangle=\alpha_{x}\langle S|u\rangle
.
Thus, by recurrence on the length,
\langle S|x_{1}\ldots x_{k}\rangle=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}\alpha_{x_{i}}

showing that
S
is a
\mathop{\tt conc}
-character. Conversely, by Schützenberger’s reconstruction
lemma,
i.e.
for any series
S
one has

S=\langle S|1_{{\mathcal{X}}^{*}}\rangle.1_{A}+\sum\limits_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}x.x^{-1}S.

But, if
S
is a
\mathop{\tt conc}
-character (
i.e.

\langle S|1_{{\mathcal{X}}^{*}}\rangle=1
and
x^{-1}S=\langle S|x\rangle S
)
then the previous expression reads
S=1_{A}+\Big(\sum\limits_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\langle S|x\rangle x\Big)S
. The last
equality is equivalent to
S=\Big(\sum\limits_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\langle S|x\rangle x\Big)^{*}
proving the claim.

(4)

As
(A\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle,\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}})
and
(A\langle Y\rangle,\;\begin{picture}(220.0,580.0)\put(220.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(220.0,582.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{20.0}}\put(240.0,582.0){\line{ 0}{ 1}{10.0}}\put(230.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(225.0,587.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{10.0}}\end{picture}\;)
are enveloping algebras,
this property is an application of the fact that, on an enveloping
\mathcal{U}
, the
characters are linearly independent with respect to the convolution algebra
\mathcal{U}^{*}_{\infty}

(see the general construction and proof in
[
26
]
. Here, this convolution algebra
(
\mathcal{U}^{*}_{\infty}
) contains the polynomials (is equal in case of finite
{\mathcal{X}}
).
Now, consider a monomial

(\varphi_{i_{1}}^{*})^{\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}\alpha_{1}}\ldots(\varphi_{i_{n}}^{*})^{\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}\alpha_{n}}=\Big(\sum\limits_{k=1}^{n}\alpha_{i_{k}}\varphi_{i_{k}}\Big)^{*}
.
The
{\mathbb{Z}}
-linear independence of the monomials in
(\varphi_{i})_{i\in I}

implies that all these monomials are linearly independent over
A\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle

which proves algebraic independence of the family
(\varphi_{i})_{i\in I}
.

To end with, the fact that
\mathcal{L}yn({\mathcal{X}})\uplus\{\varphi_{i}^{*}\}_{i\in I}
is algebraically free comes
from Radford theorem
(A\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle,\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}},1_{{\mathcal{X}}^{*}})\simeq A[\mathcal{L}yn({\mathcal{X}})]
and the transitivity
of polynomial algebras (see
[
2
]
).

(5)

Comes directly as an application of the preceding point.

∎

Remark 2.16
.

(1)

The last inclusion of item
1
is strict as shows the example
of the following identity, living in
A_{\mathrm{exc}}^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle Y\rangle\rangle
but not in

A_{\mathrm{exc}}^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle Y\rangle\rangle\cap A_{\mathrm{fin}}^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle Y\rangle\rangle

\displaystyle(ty_{1}+t^{2}y_{2}+\ldots)^{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow+\infty}(ty_{1}+\ldots+t^{k}y_{k})^{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow+\infty}(ty_{1})^{*}\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}\ldots\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}(t^{k}y_{k})^{*}=\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}_{k\geq 1}(t^{k}y_{k})^{*}.

(2)

Item
2
can be rephrased in terms of stars
as
A^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle x\rangle\rangle=\{P(xQ)^{*}\}_{P,Q\in A[x]}
holds for every ring and is therefore
characteristic free, unlike the shuffle version requiring algebraic closure and denominators.

Corollary 2.17

(Kleene stars of the plane)
.

Let
R
and
L\in A^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle
, such that
\langle R|1_{{\mathcal{X}}^{*}}\rangle=1_{A}
,
\langle L|1_{{\mathcal{X}}^{*}}\rangle=0
and
L^{*}=R
. Then the following assertions are equivalent.

(1)

R
is a
\mathop{\tt conc}
-character of
(A\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle,\mathop{\tt conc},1_{{\mathcal{X}}^{*}})
.

(2)

There is a family of coefficients
(c_{x})_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}
such that

\displaystyle R=\Big(\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}c_{x}x\Big)^{*}.

(3)

The series
R
admits a linear representation of dimension one
17
17

17

The dimension is here (as in
[
1
]
) the size of the matrices.
.

(4)

L
belongs to the plane
{A.{\mathcal{X}}}
.

(5)

L
is an infinitesimal
\mathop{\tt conc}
-character of
(A\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle,\mathop{\tt conc},1_{X^{*}})
.

Proof.

1

\Leftrightarrow

2
:

This corresponds to the point
3
of Theorem
2.15
above.

2

\Leftrightarrow

3

This is a direct consequence of Theorem
2.7
.

2

\Leftrightarrow

4
:

This is obvious, by construction (in which
L
is viewed as the

\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}
-logarithm of
R
). Indeed, since
(c_{x}x)^{n}=\dfrac{(c_{x}x)^{\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}n}}{n!}

for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, doing as in Remark
2.16
, one has

\displaystyle R=\Big(\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}c_{x}x\Big)^{*}=\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}(c_{x}x)^{*}=\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\exp_{\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}}(c_{x}x)=\exp_{\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}}\Big(\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}c_{x}x\Big).

4

\Leftrightarrow

5
:

If
L
is an infinitesimal character then, by Definition
2.5
,

\langle L|uv\rangle=\langle L|u\rangle\langle v|1_{{\mathcal{X}}^{*}}\rangle+\langle u|1_{{\mathcal{X}}^{*}}\rangle\langle L|v\rangle,

for
u,v\in{\mathcal{X}}^{*}
.
Hence, for
w=uv\in{\mathcal{X}}^{\geq 2}
with
u,v\in{\mathcal{X}}^{+}
, one gets
\langle L|w\rangle=\langle L|uv\rangle=0
.
In addition, for
u=v=1_{{\mathcal{X}}^{*}}
, one also gets
\langle L|1_{{\mathcal{X}}^{*}}\rangle=0
and it follows that

L=\sum\limits_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\langle L|x\rangle x
. Conversely, since for any
u,v\in{\mathcal{X}}^{+}
and
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
,
one has

\langle uv|x\rangle=\langle u|x\rangle\langle v|1_{{\mathcal{X}}^{*}}\rangle+\langle u|1_{{\mathcal{X}}^{*}}\rangle\langle v|x\rangle=0

then, by the pairing in (
2.5
), one deduces that

\langle L|uv\rangle=\sum\limits_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\langle L|x\rangle\langle uv|x\rangle=0

meaning that
L
is an infinitesimal
\mathop{\tt conc}
-character.
∎

Proposition 2.19
.

Let
\{\alpha_{x}\}_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}},\{\beta_{x}\}_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}
and
\{a_{s}\}_{s\geq 1},\{b_{s}\}_{s\geq 1}
be complex numbers. Then

\displaystyle\Big(\sum\limits_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\alpha_{x}x\Big)^{*}\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}\Big(\sum\limits_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\beta_{x}x\Big)^{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big(\sum\limits_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}(\alpha_{x}+\beta_{x})x\Big)^{*},

\displaystyle\Big(\sum\limits_{s\geq 1}a_{s}y_{s}\Big)^{*}\;\begin{picture}(220.0,580.0)\put(220.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(220.0,582.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{20.0}}\put(240.0,582.0){\line{ 0}{ 1}{10.0}}\put(230.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(225.0,587.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{10.0}}\end{picture}\;\Big(\sum\limits_{s\geq 1}b_{s}y_{s}\Big)^{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big(\sum\limits_{s\geq 1}(a_{s}+b_{s})y_{s}+\sum\limits_{r,s\geq 1}a_{s}b_{r}y_{s+r}\Big)^{*}.

Proof.

Using (
2.4
), for any
x_{i}\in{\mathcal{X}}
, one has

\displaystyle\langle\Big(\sum\limits_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\alpha_{x}x\Big)^{*}\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}\Big(\sum\limits_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\beta_{x}x\Big)^{*}|x_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\Big(\sum\limits_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\alpha_{x}x\Big)^{*}\otimes\Big(\sum\limits_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\beta_{x}x\Big)^{*}|\Delta_{\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}}(x_{i})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\Big(\sum\limits_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\alpha_{x}x\Big)^{*}\otimes\Big(\sum\limits_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\beta_{x}x\Big)^{*}|x_{i}\otimes 1_{X^{*}}+1_{X^{*}}\otimes x_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle=\alpha_{i}+\beta_{i}

\displaystyle=\langle\Big(\sum\limits_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}(\alpha_{x}+\beta_{x})x\Big)^{*}|x_{i}\rangle.

Using (
2.10
), for any
y_{t}\in Y
, one also has

\displaystyle\langle\Bigl(\sum_{s\geq 1}a_{s}y_{s}\Bigr)^{*}\;\begin{picture}(220.0,580.0)\put(220.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(220.0,582.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{20.0}}\put(240.0,582.0){\line{ 0}{ 1}{10.0}}\put(230.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(225.0,587.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{10.0}}\end{picture}\;\Bigl(\sum_{s\geq 1}b_{s}y_{s}\Bigr)^{*}|y_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\Bigl(\sum_{s\geq 1}a_{s}y_{s}\Bigr)^{*}\otimes\Bigl(\sum_{s\geq 1}b_{s}y_{s}\Bigr)^{*}|\Delta_{\;\begin{picture}(220.0,580.0)\put(220.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(220.0,582.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{20.0}}\put(240.0,582.0){\line{ 0}{ 1}{10.0}}\put(230.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(225.0,587.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{10.0}}\end{picture}\;}(y_{t})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\Bigl(\sum_{s\geq 1}a_{s}y_{s}\Bigr)^{*}\otimes\Bigl(\sum_{s\geq 1}b_{s}y_{s}\Bigr)^{*}|y_{t}\otimes 1_{Y^{*}}+1_{Y^{*}}\otimes y_{t}+\sum_{r,s\geq 1,r+s=t}y_{s}\otimes y_{r}\rangle

\displaystyle=a_{t}+b_{t}+\sum_{r,s\geq 1,r+s=t}a_{s}b_{r}

\displaystyle=\langle\Bigl(\sum_{s\geq 1}(a_{s}+b_{s})y_{s}+\sum_{r,s\geq 1}a_{s}b_{r}y_{s+r}\Bigr)^{*}|y_{t}\rangle.

∎

Corollary 2.20
.

Let
x\in{\mathcal{X}},y_{k}\in Y
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\geq 1}
. Then

\displaystyle(x^{*})^{\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}n}=(nx)^{*}

and

\displaystyle(y_{k}^{*})^{\;\begin{picture}(220.0,580.0)\put(220.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(220.0,582.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{20.0}}\put(240.0,582.0){\line{ 0}{ 1}{10.0}}\put(230.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(225.0,587.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{10.0}}\end{picture}\;n}=\Big(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}(n-i+1)y_{ik}\Big)^{*}.

Proof.

It is immediate for
n=1
and supposed true up to rank
n\geq 1
.
Next, since
(x^{*})^{\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}n+1}=x^{*}\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}(x^{*})^{\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}n}
and

(y_{k}^{*})^{\;\begin{picture}(220.0,580.0)\put(220.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(220.0,582.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{20.0}}\put(240.0,582.0){\line{ 0}{ 1}{10.0}}\put(230.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(225.0,587.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{10.0}}\end{picture}\;n+1}=y_{k}^{*}\;\begin{picture}(220.0,580.0)\put(220.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(220.0,582.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{20.0}}\put(240.0,582.0){\line{ 0}{ 1}{10.0}}\put(230.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(225.0,587.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{10.0}}\end{picture}\;(y_{k}^{*})^{\;\begin{picture}(220.0,580.0)\put(220.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(220.0,582.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{20.0}}\put(240.0,582.0){\line{ 0}{ 1}{10.0}}\put(230.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(225.0,587.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{10.0}}\end{picture}\;n}
then by hypothesis and then,
by Proposition
2.19
, one deduces that
(x^{*})^{\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}n+1}=((n+1)x)^{*}
and also

\displaystyle(y_{k}^{*})^{\;\begin{picture}(220.0,580.0)\put(220.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(220.0,582.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{20.0}}\put(240.0,582.0){\line{ 0}{ 1}{10.0}}\put(230.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(225.0,587.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{10.0}}\end{picture}\;n+1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle y_{k}^{*}\;\begin{picture}(220.0,580.0)\put(220.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(220.0,582.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{20.0}}\put(240.0,582.0){\line{ 0}{ 1}{10.0}}\put(230.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(225.0,587.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{10.0}}\end{picture}\;\Big(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}(n-i+1)y_{ik}\Big)^{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big((n+1)y_{k}^{*}+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}(n-i+1))y_{(i+1)k}\Big)^{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n}(n+1-i)y_{(i+1)k}\Big)^{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}(n-i)y_{ik}\Big)^{*}.

∎

Theorem 2.22

(Triangular sub bialgebras of
(A^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle,\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}},1_{X^{*}},\Delta_{\mathop{\tt conc}})
,
[
41
]
)
.

Let
\rho=(\nu,\mu,\eta)
a representation of
R\in A^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle
. Then

(1)

If
\{\mu(x)\}_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}
mutually commute
and if the alphabet is finite, any rational exchangeable series decomposes as

R=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}\limits_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}R_{x}^{(i)}
, with
R_{x}^{(i)}\in A^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle x\rangle\rangle
.

(2)

If
{\mathcal{L}}
consists of upper-triangular matrices then
R\in{A_{\mathrm{exc}}^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle}\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}A\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle
.

(3)

Let
M(x):=\mu(x)x
, for
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
. Then
M(R)=\sum\limits_{w\in{\mathcal{X}}^{*}}\langle R|w\rangle\mu(w)w
and then
R=\nu{M({\mathcal{X}}^{*})}\eta
.
Moreover,

(a)

Since
A
contains
{\mathbb{Q}}
then, by (
2.6
), one has
M({\mathcal{X}}^{*})=\prod\limits_{l\in\mathcal{L}yn{\mathcal{X}}}^{\searrow}e^{S_{l}\mu(P_{l})}
.

By (
2.13
), one has in addition,
M(Y^{*})=\prod\limits_{l\in\mathcal{L}ynY}^{\searrow}e^{\Sigma_{l}\mu(\Pi_{l})}
.

(b)

If
\{\mu(x)\}_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}
are upper-triangular then there exists a diagonal (resp. strictly upper-triangular) letter matrix

D({\mathcal{X}})
(resp.
N({\mathcal{X}})
) such that
M({\mathcal{X}})=D({\mathcal{X}})+N({\mathcal{X}})
and then, by Lazard factorization
[
47
]
, one has

M({\mathcal{X}}^{*})=((D({\mathcal{X}}^{*})N({\mathcal{X}}))^{*}D({\mathcal{X}}^{*}))
.

(c)

For
X=\{x_{0},x_{1}\}
, similarly (by Lazard factorization again),

\displaystyle M((x_{0}+x_{1})^{*})=(M(x_{1}^{*})M(x_{0}))^{*}M(x_{1}^{*})=(M(x_{0}^{*})M(x_{1}))^{*}M(x_{0}^{*})

and the modules generated by the families series in (
2.21
)–(
2.23
) are closed by
\mathop{\tt conc}
and
\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}
.

Furthermore, it follows that
R
is a linear combination of series in (
2.21
) (resp. (
2.22
)) if
M(x_{1}^{*})M(x_{0})

(resp.
M(x_{0}^{*})M(x_{1})
) is strictly upper-triangular.

In order to establish Theorem
2.24
below, we will use the following

Lemma 2.23
.

Let
(\nu,\tau,\eta)
a representation of
S
of dimension
r
such that, for all

x\in{\mathcal{X}}
, (
\tau(x)-c(x)I_{r}
) is strictly upper triangular, then

S\in K_{\mathrm{exc}}^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}K\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle
.

Proof.

Let
(e_{i})_{1\leq i\leq r}
be the canonical basis of
M_{1,r}(K)
.
We construct the representations
\rho_{1}=(\nu,(x\longmapsto\tau(x)-c(x)I_{r}),\eta)
,

\rho_{2}=(e_{1},(x\longmapsto c(x)I_{r}),{}^{t}e_{1})
of
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
and remark that

S_{1}\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}S_{2}
admits the representation

\rho_{3}=(\nu\otimes e_{1},((\tau(x)-c(x)I_{r})\otimes I_{r}+I_{r}\otimes c(x)I_{r})_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}},\eta\otimes{}^{t}e_{1})

as
I_{r}\otimes c(x)I_{r}=c(x)I_{r}\otimes I_{r}
,
\rho_{3}
is, in fact,

(\nu\otimes e_{1},(\tau(x)\otimes I_{r})_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}},\eta\otimes{}^{t}e_{1})

which represents
S
, the result now comes from the fact that
S_{1}\in K\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle

and
S_{2}=\Big(\sum\limits_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}c(x)x\Big)^{*}\in K_{\mathrm{exc}}^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle
.
∎

We first begin by properties essentially true over algebraically closed fields.

Theorem 2.24

(Triangular sub bialgebras of
(K^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle,\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}},1_{X^{*}},\Delta_{\mathop{\tt conc}})
,
[
41
]
)
.

We suppose that
K
is an algebraically closed field and that
\rho=(\nu,\mu,\eta)

is a linear representation of
R\in K^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle
of minimal dimension
n
,
we note
{\mathcal{L}}={\mathcal{L}}(\mu)\subset K^{n\times n}
the Lie algebra generated by the matrices

(\mu(x))_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}
. Then

(1)

{\mathcal{L}}
is commutative if and only if
R\in K_{\mathrm{exc}}^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle
,

(2)

{\mathcal{L}}
is nilpotent if and only if

R\in{K_{\mathrm{exc}}^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle}\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}K\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle
,

(3)

{\mathcal{L}}
is solvable if and only if
R
is a linear combination of expressions in the form
(F_{2})
.

Moreover, denoting
K_{\mathrm{nil}}^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle
(resp.
K_{\mathrm{sol}}^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle
),
the set of rational series such that
{\mathcal{L}}(\mu)
is nilpotent (resp. solvable), we get a tower of sub Hopf algebras of
the Sweedler’s dual,
K_{\mathrm{nil}}^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle\subset K_{\mathrm{sol}}^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle\subset{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}}^{\circ}({\mathcal{X}})
.

Proof.

(1)

Let us remark that, for
x,y\in{\mathcal{X}},p,s\in{\mathcal{X}}^{*}
, we have

\langle R|pxys\rangle=\langle R|pyxs\rangle
which is due to the commutation of matrices.
Conversely, since
\rho
is minimal then there is
P_{i},Q_{i}\in K\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle,i=1...n

such that

\mu(u)=(\langle P_{i}\triangleright R\triangleleft Q_{i}|u\rangle)_{1\leq i,j\leq n}=(\langle R|Q_{i}uP_{i}\rangle)_{1\leq i,j\leq n},

for
u\in{\mathcal{X}}^{*}
(see
[
1
,
19
]
).
Now,

\mu(xy)=(\langle R|Q_{i}xyP_{i}\rangle)_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{=}}(\langle R|Q_{i}yxP_{i}\rangle)_{1\leq i,j\leq n}=\mu(yx),

for
x,y\in{\mathcal{X}}
(equality
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{=}}
being due to exchangeability).

(2)

Let
K^{n}
be the space of the representation of
{\mathcal{L}}
given by
\mu
and
\bigoplus\limits_{j=1}^{m}V_{j}

be a decomposition of
K^{n}
into indecomposable
{\mathcal{L}}
-modules (see
[
17
]
for characteristic
0
, or

[
3
]
for arbitrary characteristic), we know that
V_{j}
is a
{\mathcal{L}}
-module and the action of
{\mathcal{L}}
is
triangularizable with constant diagonals inside each sector
V_{j}
. Thus, it is an invertible matrix
P
in
\mathrm{GL}(n,K)
such that

\displaystyle\forall x\in{\mathcal{X}},

\displaystyle P\mu(x)P^{-1}=\mathrm{blockdiag}(T_{1},T_{2}\ldots,T_{k})=\begin{pmatrix}T_{1}&0&0&\ldots&0\cr 0&T_{2}&0&\ldots&0\cr\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\cr 0&0&\ldots&0&T_{k}\end{pmatrix}

where the
T_{j}
are upper triangular matrices with scalar diagonal
i.e.
is of the form

T_{j}(x)=\lambda(x)I+N(x)
where
N(x)
is strictly upper-triangular
20
20

20

Even, as
K
is infinite,
there is a global linear form on
{\mathcal{L}}
,
\lambda_{lin}
such that, for all
g\in{\mathcal{L}}
,

PgP^{-1}-\lambda_{lin}(g)I
is strictly upper-triangular.
. Set
d_{j}
to be the dimension of
T_{j}

(so that
n=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m}\,d_{j}
), partitioning
\nu P^{-1}=\nu^{\prime}
(resp.
P\eta=\eta^{\prime}
)
with these dimensions we get blocks so that each
(\nu^{\prime}_{j},T_{j},\eta^{\prime}_{j})
is the representation
of a series
R_{j}
and
R=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m}\,R_{j}
. It suffices then to prove that any

R_{j}\in K_{\mathrm{exc}}^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}K\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle
.
This is a consequence of Lemma
2.23
.

Conversely, if
\rho_{i}=(\nu_{i},\tau_{i},\eta_{i}),i=1,2
, are two representations then

[\tau_{1}(x)\otimes I_{r}+I_{r}\otimes\tau_{2}(x),\tau_{1}(y)\otimes I_{r}+I_{r}\otimes\tau_{2}(y)]=[\tau_{1}(x),\tau_{1}(y)]\otimes I_{r}+I_{r}\otimes[\tau_{2}(x),\tau_{2}(y)]

and a similar formula holds for
m
-fold brackets (Dynkin combs), so that if
{\mathcal{L}}(\tau_{i})
’s
are nilpotent, the Lie algebra
{\mathcal{L}}(\tau_{1}\otimes I_{r}+I_{r}\otimes\tau_{2})
is also nilpotent.
The point here comes from the fact that series in
K_{\mathrm{exc}}^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle

as well as in
K\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle
admit nilpotent representations, so, let
(\alpha,\tau,\beta)

such a representation and
(\alpha^{\prime},\tau^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
its minimal quotient (obtained by minimization,
see
[
1
]
), then
{\mathcal{L}}(\tau^{\prime})
is nilpotent as a quotient of
{\mathcal{L}}(\tau)
.
Now two minimal representations being isomorphic,
{\mathcal{L}}(\mu)
is isomorphic to
{\mathcal{L}}(\tau)

and then it is nilpotent.

(3)

As
{\mathcal{L}}
is solvable and
K
is algebraically closed, by Lie’s theorem,
we can find a conjugate form of
\rho=(\nu,\mu,\eta)
such that
\mu(x)
s are upper-triangular.
Since this form also represents
R
, letting
D({\mathcal{X}})
(resp.
N({\mathcal{X}})
) be the diagonal
(resp. strictly upper-triangular) letter matrice such that
M({\mathcal{X}})=D({\mathcal{X}})+N({\mathcal{X}})
then

R=\nu M({\mathcal{X}}^{*})\eta=\nu(D({\mathcal{X}}^{*})N({\mathcal{X}}))^{*}D({\mathcal{X}}^{*})\eta.

Since
D({\mathcal{X}}^{*})N({\mathcal{X}})
is nilpotent of order
n
then

(D({\mathcal{X}}^{*})N({\mathcal{X}}))^{*}=\sum\limits_{j=0}^{n}(D({\mathcal{X}}^{*})N({\mathcal{X}}))^{j}.

Hence, letting
{{\mathcal{S}}}
be the vector space generated by forms of type
(F_{2})
which is
closed by concatenation, we have
D({\mathcal{X}}^{*})N({\mathcal{X}})\in{{\mathcal{S}}}^{n\times n}
and then

(D({\mathcal{X}}^{*})N({\mathcal{X}}))^{*}\in{{\mathcal{S}}}^{n\times n}
.
Finally,
R=\nu M({\mathcal{X}}^{*})\eta\in{{\mathcal{S}}}
which is the claim.

Conversely, as sums and quotients of solvable representations
are solvable is suffices to show that a single form of type
F_{2}
admits a solvable representation
and end by quotient and isomorphism as in (ii). From Proposition (
2.10
),
we get the fact that, if
R_{i}
admit solvable representations so does
R_{1}R_{2}
,
then the claim follows from the fact that, firstly, single letters admit solvable (even nilpotent)
representations and secondly series of
\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}\limits_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\{K^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle x\rangle\rangle\}

admit solvable representations. Finally, we choose (or construct) a solvable representation of
R
,
call it
(\alpha,\tau,\beta)
and
(\alpha^{\prime},\tau^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
its minimal quotient,
then
{\mathcal{L}}(\tau^{\prime})
is solvable as a quotient of
{\mathcal{L}}(\tau)
. Now two minimal representations
being isomorphic,
{\mathcal{L}}(\mu)
is isomorphic to
{\mathcal{L}}(\tau)
, hence solvable.

∎

Remark 2.25
.

For an example of series
S
with solvable representation but such that

S\notin K_{\mathrm{exc}}^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\rangle\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}K\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle
,
one can take
{\mathcal{X}}=\{a,b\}
and
S=a^{*}b(-a)^{*}
.

3.
Indexation of polylogarithms and harmonic sums

3.1.
Indexation by noncommutative generating series

Any integral multi-indices
(s_{1},\ldots,s_{r})\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\geq 1}^{r},r\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\geq 1},
can be associated
with
x_{0}^{s_{1}-1}x_{1}\ldots x_{0}^{s_{r}-1}x_{1}\in X^{*}x_{1}
. Similarly, any
(s_{1},\ldots,s_{r})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}

can be associated with the word
y_{s_{1}}\ldots y_{s_{r}}\in Y_{0}^{*}
.
Let then
\mathrm{Li}_{x_{0}^{r}}(z)={(\log(z))^{r}}/{r!}
and let
\mathrm{Li}_{s_{1},\ldots,s_{k}}
(resp.
\mathrm{Li}_{-s_{1},\ldots,-s_{k}}
)
and
\mathrm{H}_{s_{1},\ldots,s_{k}}
(resp.
\mathrm{H}_{-s_{1},\ldots,-s_{k}}
) be indexed by words,
i.e.

(3.1)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Li}_{x_{0}^{s_{1}-1}x_{1}\ldots x_{0}^{s_{r}-1}x_{1}}=\mathrm{Li}_{s_{1},\ldots,s_{r}}

\displaystyle(\mbox{resp.}

\displaystyle\mathrm{Li}^{-}_{y_{s_{1}}\ldots y_{s_{r}}}=\mathrm{Li}_{-s_{1},\ldots,-s_{r}}),

(3.2)

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{y_{s_{1}}\ldots y_{s_{r}}}=\mathrm{H}_{s_{1},\ldots,s_{r}}

\displaystyle(\mbox{resp.}

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{-}_{y_{s_{1}}\ldots y_{s_{r}}}=\mathrm{H}_{-s_{1},\ldots,-s_{r}}).

In particular,
\mathrm{Li}^{-}_{y_{0}^{r}}(z)=({z}/(1-z))^{r}

and
\mathrm{H}^{-}_{y_{0}^{r}}(n)=\binom{n}{r}=(n)_{r}/r!
.

Theorem 3.1

(
[
34
,
36
]
)
.

The following morphisms of algebras are injective

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{\bullet}:({\mathbb{Q}}\langle Y\rangle,\;\begin{picture}(220.0,580.0)\put(220.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(220.0,582.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{20.0}}\put(240.0,582.0){\line{ 0}{ 1}{10.0}}\put(230.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(225.0,587.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{10.0}}\end{picture}\;,1_{Y^{*}})\longrightarrow({\mathbb{Q}}\{\mathrm{H}_{w}\}_{w\in Y^{*}},\times,1),

\displaystyle w\longmapsto\mathrm{H}_{w},

\displaystyle\mathrm{Li}_{\bullet}:(\mathbb{Q}\langle X\rangle,\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}},1_{X^{*}})\longrightarrow({\mathbb{Q}}\{\mathrm{Li}_{w}\}_{w\in X^{*}},\times,1_{\tilde{B}}),

\displaystyle w\longmapsto\mathrm{Li}_{w}

Hence,
\{\mathrm{H}_{w}\}_{w\in Y^{*}}
(resp.
\{\mathrm{H}_{l}\}_{l\in\mathcal{L}ynY}
) and
\{\mathrm{Li}_{w}\}_{w\in X^{*}}

(resp.
\{\mathrm{Li}_{l}\}_{l\in\mathcal{L}ynX}
) are
{\mathbb{Q}}
-linearly (resp.algebraically ) independent.

Theorem 3.2

(
[
22
]
)
.

There exists also a law of algebra, denoted by
\top
, in
{\mathbb{Q}}\langle\!\langle Y_{0}\rangle\!\rangle
such that
the following morphisms of algebras are surjective

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{-}_{\bullet}:({\mathbb{Q}}\langle Y_{0}\rangle,\;\begin{picture}(220.0,580.0)\put(220.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(220.0,582.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{20.0}}\put(240.0,582.0){\line{ 0}{ 1}{10.0}}\put(230.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(225.0,587.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{10.0}}\end{picture}\;,1_{Y_{0}^{*}})\longrightarrow({\mathbb{Q}}\{\mathrm{H}^{-}_{w}\}_{w\in Y_{0}^{*}},\times,1),

\displaystyle w\longmapsto\mathrm{H}^{-}_{w},

\displaystyle\mathrm{Li}^{-}_{\bullet}:({\mathbb{Q}}\langle Y_{0}\rangle,\top,1_{Y_{0}^{*}})\longrightarrow({\mathbb{Q}}\{\mathrm{Li}^{-}_{w}\}_{w\in Y_{0}^{*}},\times,1_{\Omega}),

Then
\ker\mathrm{H}^{-}_{\bullet}=\ker\mathrm{Li}^{-}_{\bullet}={\mathbb{Q}}\langle\{w-w\top 1_{Y_{0}^{*}}\mid w\in Y_{0}^{*}\}\rangle

and the families
\{\mathrm{H}^{-}_{y_{k}}\}_{k\geq 0}
and
\{\mathrm{Li}^{-}_{y_{k}}\}_{k\geq 0}
are
{\mathbb{Q}}
-linearly independent.
Moreover, let
\top^{\prime}:{{\mathbb{Q}}}\langle Y_{0}\rangle\times{{\mathbb{Q}}}\langle Y_{0}\rangle\rightarrow{{\mathbb{Q}}}\langle Y_{0}\rangle

be a law such that
\mathrm{Li}^{-}_{\bullet}
is a morphism for
\top^{\prime}
and

(1_{Y_{0}^{*}}\top^{\prime}{{\mathbb{Q}}}\langle Y_{0}\rangle)\cap\ker(\mathrm{Li}^{-}_{\bullet})=\{0\}
.
Then
\top^{\prime}=g\circ\top
, where
g\in GL({{\mathbb{Q}}}\langle Y_{0}\rangle)

is such that
\mathrm{Li}^{-}_{\bullet}\circ g=\mathrm{Li}^{-}_{\bullet}
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}
denotes the group of transformations, generated by
\{z\mapsto 1-z,z\mapsto 1/z\}
,
permuting the singularities in
\{0,1,+\infty\}
.
Let us also consider the differential rings (considered as a subring of
{\mathcal{H}}(\Omega)
)

(3.3)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}_{0}:={\mathbb{C}}[z,z^{-1}],

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}_{1}:={\mathbb{C}}[z,(1-z)^{-1}],

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}:={\mathbb{C}}[z,z^{-1},(1-z)^{-1}],

(3.4)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}_{0}^{\prime}:={\mathbb{C}}[z^{-1}],

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}_{1}^{\prime}:={\mathbb{C}}[(1-z)^{-1}],

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}:={\mathbb{C}}[z^{-1},(1-z)^{-1}].

Then we have

Lemma 3.3

(
[

]

(1)

For any

and

(2)

For any

(3)

The morphism

The following morphisms of algebras are bijective

(

.

(1)

\forall x_{i}\in X,{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\!\langle x_{i}\rangle\!\rangle=\mathrm{span}_{{\mathbb{C}}}\{(tx_{i})^{*}\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}{{\mathbb{C}}}\langle x_{i}\rangle|{t\in{\mathbb{C}}}\}

Proposition 3.5

(

.

Let

.

.

Now, let us consider generating series of polylogarithms and of harmonic sums.

Theorem 3.6

(
[
34
,

]
)
.

The graphs of
\mathrm{Li}_{\bullet}
and

, defined in Theorem

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}=\sum_{w\in X^{*}}\mathrm{Li}_{w}w=(\mathrm{H}_{\bullet}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{Y}){\mathcal{D}}_{\;\begin{picture}(220.0,580.0)\put(220.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(220.0,582.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{20.0}}\put(240.0,582.0){\line{ 0}{ 1}{10.0}}\put(230.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(225.0,587.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{10.0}}\end{picture}\;}

and

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}=\sum_{w\in Y^{*}}\mathrm{H}_{w}w=(\mathrm{Li}_{\bullet}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{X}){\mathcal{D}}_{X}.

Then
\Delta_{\;\begin{picture}(220.0,580.0)\put(220.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(220.0,582.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{20.0}}\put(240.0,582.0){\line{ 0}{ 1}{10.0}}\put(230.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(225.0,587.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{10.0}}\end{picture}\;}(\mathrm{H})=\mathrm{H}\otimes\mathrm{H},\Delta_{\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}}(\mathrm{L})=\mathrm{L}\otimes\mathrm{L}

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}=\prod_{l\in\mathcal{L}ynY}^{\searrow}e^{\mathrm{H}_{\Sigma_{l}}\Pi_{l}}

and

\displaystyle\log(\mathrm{H})=\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{(-1)^{k-1}}{k}\sum_{u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}\in Y^{+}}\mathrm{H}_{u_{1}\;\begin{picture}(220.0,580.0)\put(220.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(220.0,582.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{20.0}}\put(240.0,582.0){\line{ 0}{ 1}{10.0}}\put(230.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(225.0,587.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{10.0}}\end{picture}\;\ldots\;\begin{picture}(220.0,580.0)\put(220.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(220.0,582.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{20.0}}\put(240.0,582.0){\line{ 0}{ 1}{10.0}}\put(230.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(225.0,587.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{10.0}}\end{picture}\;u_{k}}\;u_{1}\ldots u_{k},

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}=\prod_{l\in\mathcal{L}ynX}^{\searrow}e^{\mathrm{Li}_{S_{l}}P_{l}}

and

\displaystyle\log(\mathrm{L})=\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{(-1)^{k-1}}{k}\sum_{u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}\in X^{+}}\mathrm{Li}_{u_{1}\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}\ldots\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}u_{k}}\;u_{1}\ldots u_{k}.

One can then set the following series

(3.5)

\displaystyle Z_{\;\begin{picture}(220.0,580.0)\put(220.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(220.0,582.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{20.0}}\put(240.0,582.0){\line{ 0}{ 1}{10.0}}\put(230.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(225.0,587.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{10.0}}\end{picture}\;}:=\prod_{l\in\mathcal{L}ynY\setminus\{y_{1}\}}^{\searrow}e^{\mathrm{H}_{\Sigma_{l}}(+\infty)\Pi_{l}}

and

\displaystyle Z_{\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}}:=\prod_{l\in\mathcal{L}ynX\setminus X}^{\searrow}e^{\mathrm{Li}_{S_{l}}(1)P_{l}}.

By term wise differentiation, the series
\mathrm{L}
in Theorem

satisfies (

.

\displaystyle\zeta:{\displaystyle({\mathbb{Q}}1_{X^{*}}\oplus x_{0}\mathbb{Q}\langle X\rangle x_{1},\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}},1_{X^{*}})\atop\displaystyle({\mathbb{Q}}1_{Y^{*}}\oplus(Y\setminus\{y_{1}\}){\mathbb{Q}}\langle Y\rangle,\;\begin{picture}(220.0,580.0)\put(220.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(220.0,582.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{20.0}}\put(240.0,582.0){\line{ 0}{ 1}{10.0}}\put(230.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(225.0,587.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{10.0}}\end{picture}\;,1_{Y^{*}})}

\displaystyle\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\twoheadrightarrow

\displaystyle({\mathcal{Z}},\times,1),

\displaystyle{\displaystyle x_{0}x_{1}^{s_{1}-1}\ldots x_{0}x_{1}^{s_{k}-1}\atop\displaystyle y_{s_{1}}\ldots y_{s_{k}}}

3.2.
Global asymptotic behaviors by noncommutative generating series

Singularities analysis on the coefficients of the noncommutative
generating series of
\{\mathrm{Li}_{w}\}_{w\in X^{*}}
, putted in the factorized
form (see Theorem
3.6
) leads to the following asymptotic behavior
[
34
]

(3.7)

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}(z)\sim_{0}\exp(x_{0}\log z)

and

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}(z)\sim_{1}\exp(-x_{1}\log(1-z))Z_{\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}}.

In
[
24
,
41
]
, the profs of the uniqueness of the series
\mathrm{L}

and
Z_{\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}}
(
i.e.

\Phi_{KZ}
) are also given.
Via an identity of the type Newton-Girard, we obtain
[
14
,
15
]

(3.8)

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}(n)\sim_{+\infty}\sum_{k\geq 0}\mathrm{H}_{y_{1}^{k}}y_{1}^{k}\pi_{Y}(Z_{\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}})

and

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}\mathrm{H}_{y_{1}^{k}}y_{1}^{k}=e^{\sum_{k\geq 1}\mathrm{H}_{y_{k}}(n){(-y_{1})^{k}}/k}.

In other terms, we have the following global renormalization

Theorem 3.7

(first Abel like theorem,
[
14
,
15
]
)
.

Let

\displaystyle\pi_{Y}:(A1_{X^{*}}\oplus A\langle X\rangle x_{1},{\tt conc},1_{Y^{*}})\rightarrow(A\langle Y\rangle,{\tt conc},1_{Y^{*}})

be the morphism, mapping
x_{0}^{s_{1}-1}x_{1}\ldots x_{0}^{s_{r}-1}x_{1}
to
y_{s_{1}}\ldots y_{s_{r}}
. Then

\displaystyle\lim_{z\rightarrow 1}e^{y_{1}\log(1-z)}\pi_{Y}(\mathrm{L}(z))=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}e^{\sum_{k\geq 1}\mathrm{H}_{y_{k}}(n){(-y_{1})^{k}}/k}\mathrm{H}(n)=\pi_{Y}({Z}_{{\scriptstyle\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}}}).

Thus, the coefficients
\{\langle Z_{\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}}|u\rangle\}_{u\in X^{*}}

(
i.e.

\{\zeta_{\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}}(u)\}_{u\in X^{*}}
) and

\{\langle Z_{\;\begin{picture}(220.0,580.0)\put(220.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(220.0,582.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{20.0}}\put(240.0,582.0){\line{ 0}{ 1}{10.0}}\put(230.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(225.0,587.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{10.0}}\end{picture}\;}|v\rangle\}_{v\in Y^{*}}

(

(3.9)

Example 3.8

(
[
14
,
15
]
)
.

In convergence case, one has

\displaystyle\mathrm{Li}_{2,1}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\zeta(3)+(1-z)\log(1-z)-(1-z)^{-1}-(1-z)\log^{2}(1-z)/2

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle(1-z)^{2}(-\log^{2}(1-z)+\log(1-z))/4+\ldots,

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{2,1}(n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\zeta(3)-(\log(n)+1+\gamma)/n+\log(n)/{2n}+\ldots,

one has
\mathrm{f.p.}_{z\rightarrow 1}\mathrm{Li}_{2,1}(z)=\mathrm{f.p.}_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\mathrm{H}_{2,1}(n)=\zeta(2,1)=\zeta(3)
.

In divergence case, one has

\displaystyle\mathrm{Li}_{1,2}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2-2\zeta(3)-\zeta(2)\log(1-z)-2(1-z)\log(1-z)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle(1-z)\log^{2}(1-z)+(1-z)^{2}((\log^{2}(1-z)-\log(1-z))/2+\ldots,

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{1,2}(n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\zeta(2)\gamma-2\zeta(3)+\zeta(2)\log(n)+(\zeta(2)+2)/{2n}+\ldots,

since numerically,
\zeta(2)\gamma=0.94948171111498152454556410223170493364000\ldots
, then

\displaystyle\mathrm{f.p.}_{z\rightarrow 1}\mathrm{Li}_{1,2}(z)=2-2\zeta(3)

\displaystyle\neq

\displaystyle\mathrm{f.p.}_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\mathrm{H}_{1,2}(n)=\zeta(2)\gamma-2\zeta(3).

Let
Z_{\gamma}
be the generating series of
\{\gamma_{w}\}_{w\in Y^{*}}
. For convenience, we denote

\displaystyle\mathrm{F.P.}_{z\rightarrow 1}\mathrm{L}(z)=Z_{\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}},

\displaystyle\mathrm{F.P.}_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\mathrm{H}(n)=Z_{\;\begin{picture}(220.0,580.0)\put(220.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(220.0,582.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{20.0}}\put(240.0,582.0){\line{ 0}{ 1}{10.0}}\put(230.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(225.0,587.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{10.0}}\end{picture}\;},

Similarly, asymptotic behaviors of
\{\mathrm{Li}^{-}_{w}\}_{w\in Y^{*}_{0}},\{\mathrm{H}^{-}_{w}\}_{w\in Y^{*}_{0}}

Proposition 3.14

(
[
22
]
)
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
z\in{\mathbb{C}},|z|<1
and
w\in Y_{0}^{*}
,

\mathrm{H}^{-}_{w}
and
\mathrm{Li}^{-}_{w}
are polynomial, of degree
(w)+|w|
in

{\mathbb{Q}}[n]
and
{\mathbb{Z}}[(1-z)^{-1}]
, respectively.

Hence, for any
w\in Y_{0}^{*}
, there is
C^{-}_{w}\in{\mathbb{Q}}
and
B^{-}_{w}\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{-}_{w}(n)\sim_{+\infty}n^{(w)+|w|}C^{-}_{w}

and

\displaystyle\mathrm{Li}^{-}_{w}(z)\sim_{1}(1-z)^{-(w)-|w|}B^{-}_{w},

\displaystyle C^{-}_{w}=\prod_{w=uv,v\neq 1_{Y_{0}^{*}}}((v)+|v|)^{-1}

and

\displaystyle B^{-}_{w}=((w)+|w|)!C^{-}_{w}.

Theorem 3.16

(second Abel like theorem,
[
22
]
)
.

Let us consider

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}^{-}:=\sum_{w\in Y_{0}^{*}}\mathrm{Li}^{-}_{w}w,

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{-}:=\displaystyle\sum_{w\in Y_{0}^{*}}\mathrm{H}^{-}_{w}w,

\displaystyle C^{-}:=\sum_{w\in Y_{0}^{*}}C^{-}_{w}w.

Then
\langle\mathrm{H}^{-}|1_{Y_{0}^{*}}\rangle=\langle C^{-}|1_{Y_{0}^{*}}\rangle=1,\Delta_{\;\begin{picture}(220.0,580.0)\put(220.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(220.0,582.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{20.0}}\put(240.0,582.0){\line{ 0}{ 1}{10.0}}\put(230.0,592.0){\line{ 0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(225.0,587.0){\line{ 1}{ 0}{10.0}}\end{picture}\;}(\mathrm{H}^{-})=\mathrm{H}^{-}\otimes\mathrm{H}^{-}
,

\Delta_{\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}}(C^{-})=C^{-}\otimes C^{-}
and

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{z\to 1}h^{\odot-1}((1-z)^{-1})\odot\mathrm{L}^{-}(z)=\lim\limits_{n\to+\infty}g^{\odot-1}(n)\odot\mathrm{H}^{-}(n)=C^{-},

where
\odot
denotes the Hadamard product and

\displaystyle h(t):=\sum_{w\in Y_{0}^{*}}{((w)+|w|)!}{t^{(w)+|w|}}w

and

\displaystyle g(t):=(\sum_{y\in Y_{0}}t^{(y)+1}y)^{*}.

Corollary 3.17

(
[
41
]
)
.

For any
w\in Y_{0}^{*}\setminus\{1_{Y_{0}^{*}}\}
, there exists a unique polynomial
p\in({\mathbb{Z}}[t],\times,1)

of degree
(w)+|w|
such that, for
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
z\in{\mathbb{C}},|z|<1
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Li}^{-}_{w}(z)=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{(w)+|w|}\frac{p_{k}}{(1-z)^{k}}=\sum_{k=0}^{(w)+|w|}p_{k}e^{-k\log(1-z)}

\displaystyle\in({\mathbb{Z}}[e^{-\log(1-z)}],\times,1_{{B}}),

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{-}_{w}(n)=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{(w)+|w|}p_{k}{n+k\choose k}=\sum_{k=0}^{(w)+|w|}\frac{p_{k}}{k!}(n+k)_{n}

\displaystyle\in({\mathbb{Q}}[(n+\bullet)_{n}],\times,1),

where
(n+\bullet):{\mathbb{N}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{Q}}
mapping
i
to
(n+i)_{n}=(n+i)!/n!

and
{\mathbb{Q}}[(n+\bullet)_{n}]
denotes the set of polynomials, on
n
, expanded as follows

\displaystyle\forall\pi\in{\mathbb{Q}}[(n+\bullet)_{n}],

\displaystyle\displaystyle\pi=\sum_{i=0}^{d}\pi_{k}(n+i)_{n}=\sum_{i=0}^{d}\pi_{k}\frac{(n+i)!}{n!},

\displaystyle\deg(\pi)=d.

Note that, in Corollary
3.17
, for any
w\in Y_{0}^{*}\setminus\{1_{Y_{0}^{*}}\}
,
the coefficients
\{\pi_{k}\}_{k\geq 0}
depend on
w
. One should denote
\pi_{k}=\pi_{k}^{w}
,
for
k\geq 0
, and algorithmically, for
w=y_{i}u
, one can compute the coefficients

\{\pi_{k}^{y_{i}u}\}_{k\geq 0}
as follows
[
23
]

(1)

If
i=0
then

\displaystyle\pi^{y_{0}u}_{k}:=\begin{cases}\pi_{k-1}^{u}&\mbox{for }k=(u)+|u|+1,\\
\pi_{k-1}^{u}\pi_{k}^{u}&\mbox{for }1\leq k\leq(u)+|u|,\\
-\pi_{k}^{u}&\mbox{for }k=0,\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}.\\
\end{cases}

(2)

If
i>0
then

\displaystyle\pi^{y_{i}u}_{k}:=\begin{cases}(k-1)\pi_{k-1}^{y_{i-1}u}&\mbox{for }k=(u)+|u|+i+1,\\
(k-1)\pi_{k-1}^{y_{i-1}u}-k\pi_{k}^{y_{i-1}u}&\mbox{for }2\leq k\leq(u)+|u|+i,\\
-\pi_{k}^{y_{i-1}u}&\mbox{for }k=1,\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}.\\
\end{cases}

Theorem 3.21

(extension of
\mathrm{H}_{\bullet}
,
[
9
]
)
.

For any
r\geq 1,t\in{\mathbb{C}},|t|<1
, one has

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{(t^{r}y_{r})^{*}}=\sum_{k\geq 0}\mathrm{H}_{y_{r}^{k}}t^{kr}=\exp\biggl(\sum_{k\geq 1}\mathrm{H}_{y_{kr}}\frac{(-t^{r})^{k-1}}{k}\biggr).

Moreover, for
|a_{s}|<1,|b_{s}|<1
and
|a_{s}+b_{s}|<1
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{(\sum_{s\geq 1}(a_{s}+b_{s})y_{s}+\sum_{r,s\geq 1}a_{s}b_{r}y_{s+r})^{*}}=\mathrm{H}_{(\sum_{s\geq 1}a_{s}y_{s})^{*}}\mathrm{H}_{(\sum_{s\geq 1}b_{s}y_{s})^{*}}.

Hence,
\mathrm{H}_{(a_{s}y_{s}+a_{r}y_{r}+a_{s}a_{r}y_{s+r})^{*}}=\mathrm{H}_{(a_{s}y_{s})^{*}}\mathrm{H}_{(a_{r}y_{r})^{*}}

and
\mathrm{H}_{(-a_{s}^{2}y_{2s})^{*}}=\mathrm{H}_{(a_{s}y_{s})^{*}}\mathrm{H}_{(-a_{s}y_{s})^{*}}
.

Proof.

For
t\in{\mathbb{C}},|t|<1
, since
\mathrm{Li}_{(tx_{1})^{*}}
is well defined then
so is the arithmetic function, expressed via Newton-Girard formula, for
n\geq 0
, by

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{(ty_{1})^{*}}(n)=\sum_{k\geq 0}\mathrm{H}_{y_{1}^{k}}(n)t^{k}=\exp\Bigl(-\sum_{k\geq 1}\mathrm{H}_{y_{k}}(n)\frac{(-t)^{k}}{k}\Bigr)=\prod_{l=1}^{n}\Bigl(1+\frac{t}{l}\Bigr).

Similarly,
\mathrm{H}_{(t^{r}y_{r})^{*}}
(
r\geq 2
) can be expressed, via Newton-Girard formula

[
45
]
once again and via Adam’s transform (see
[
15
]
), by

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{(t^{r}y_{r})^{*}}(n)=\sum_{k\geq 0}\mathrm{H}_{y_{r}^{k}}(n)t^{kr}=\exp\Bigl(-\sum_{k\geq 1}\mathrm{H}_{y_{kr}}(n)\frac{(-t^{r})^{k}}{k}\Bigr)=\prod_{l=1}^{N}\Bigl(1-\frac{(-t^{r})}{l^{r}}\Bigr).

Since
\|\mathrm{H}_{y_{r}}\|_{\infty}\leq\zeta(r)
then
-\sum_{k\geq 1}{\mathrm{H}_{kr}{(-t^{r})^{k}}}/{k}
is termwise dominated by
\|f_{r}\|_{\infty}
,
where
f_{r}(t)=-\sum_{k\geq 1}\zeta(kr){(-t^{r})^{k}}/k
, and then
\mathrm{H}_{(t^{r}y_{r})^{*}}
by
e^{f_{r}}
(see also proposition
3.24
bellow).
By Corollary
2.17
, it follows then last results.
∎

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\Gamma(z+1)}=e^{\gamma z}\prod_{n\geq 1}\Bigl(1+\frac{z}{n}\Bigr)e^{-\frac{z}{n}}=\exp\Bigl(\gamma z-\sum_{k\geq 2}\zeta(k)\frac{(-z)^{k}}{k}\Bigr).

From the estimations from above of the proof of Theorem

Corollary 3.23
.

For any
r\geq 2
, one has

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}\zeta(\underbrace{{r,\ldots,r}}_{k{\tt\ times}})t^{kr}=\exp\Bigl(-\sum_{k\geq 1}\zeta(kr)\frac{(-t^{r})^{k}}{k}\Bigr)=\prod_{n\geq 1}\Bigl(1-\frac{(-t^{r})}{n^{r}}\Bigr).

Using the following functional equation and Euler’s complement formula,
i.e.

(3.16)

\displaystyle\Gamma(1+z)=z\Gamma(z)

and

\displaystyle\Gamma(z)\Gamma(1-z)=\frac{\pi}{\sin(z\pi)},

and also introducing the

\displaystyle\forall a,b,z\in{\mathbb{C}},

\displaystyle\Re a>0,\Re b>0,|z|<1,

\displaystyle\mathrm{B}(z;a,b):=\int_{0}^{z}dt\;t^{a-1}(1-t)^{b-1}

and then, classically,
\mathrm{B}(a,b):=\mathrm{B}(1;a,b)={\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b)}/{\Gamma(a+b)}
.
One also has
[

,
41
]

(3.18)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Li}_{x_{0}[(ax_{0})^{*}\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}((1-b)x_{1})^{*}]}(z)=\mathrm{Li}_{x_{1}[((a-1)x_{0})^{*}\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}(-bx_{1})^{*}]}(z)=\mathrm{B}(z;a,b).

Since
\mathrm{Li}_{(tx_{1})^{*}}(z)=(1-z)^{-t}
, for
|t|<1
, then by Lemma

and Proposition

, the characters

and

can be extended algebraically as follows

Proposition 3.24

(
[

,

,
41
]
)
.

The characters
\zeta_{\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}}
and
\gamma_{\bullet}
are extended algebraically as follows:

\displaystyle\zeta_{\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}}:({\mathbb{C}}\langle X\rangle\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathrm{exc}}^{\mathrm{rat}}\langle\langle X\rangle\rangle,\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}},1_{X^{*}})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle({\mathbb{C}},.,1),

\displaystyle\forall t\in{\mathbb{C}},|t|<1,(tx_{0})^{*},(tx_{1})^{*}

by (

)–(
3.16

\displaystyle\exp\Bigl(-\sum_{k\geq 2}\zeta(2k)\frac{t^{2k}}{k}\Bigr)=\sum_{k\geq 1}(-1)^{k}\frac{(t\pi)^{2k}}{(2k+1)!}=\sum_{k\geq 2}\zeta(\overbrace{{2,\ldots,2}}^{k{\tt\ times}})(-1)^{k}t^{2k}.

On the one hand, identifying the coefficients of
t^{2k}
on the second identity, one gets

\displaystyle\frac{\zeta(\overbrace{{2,\ldots,2}}^{k{\tt\ times}})}{\pi^{2k}}=\frac{1}{(2k+1)!}\in{\mathbb{Q}}.

On the other hand, taking the logarithms and considering Taylor expansions on the first identity,
one also obtains
21
21

21

Note that Euler gave another explicit formula using Bernoulli numbers.

\displaystyle\frac{{\zeta(2k)}}{\pi^{2k}}=k\sum_{l=1}^{k}\frac{(-1)^{k+l}}{l}\sum_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{l}\geq 1\atop n_{1}+\ldots+n_{l}=k}\prod_{i=1}^{l}\frac{1}{{\Gamma(2n_{i}+2)}}\in{\mathbb{Q}}.

For example,

\displaystyle\frac{\zeta(2)}{\pi^{2}}=

\displaystyle\displaystyle 1\frac{(-1)^{1+1}}{1}\frac{1}{\Gamma(4)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{6},

\displaystyle\frac{\zeta(4)}{\pi^{4}}=

\displaystyle\displaystyle 2\Bigl(\frac{(-1)^{2+1}}{1}\frac{1}{\Gamma(6)}+\frac{(-1)^{2+2}}{2}\frac{1}{\Gamma(4)\Gamma(4)}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{90},

\displaystyle\frac{\zeta(6)}{\pi^{6}}=

\displaystyle\displaystyle 3\sum_{l=1}^{3}\frac{(-1)^{3+l}}{l}\sum_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{l}\geq 1\atop n_{1}+\ldots+n_{l}=3}\prod_{i=1}^{l}\frac{1}{{\Gamma(2n_{i}+2)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{945},

\displaystyle\frac{\zeta(8)}{\pi^{8}}=

\displaystyle\displaystyle 4\sum_{l=1}^{4}\frac{(-1)^{4+l}}{l}\sum_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{l}\geq 1\atop n_{1}+\ldots+n_{l}=4}\prod_{i=1}^{l}\frac{1}{{\Gamma(2n_{i}+2)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{9450},

\displaystyle\frac{\zeta(10)}{\pi^{10}}=

\displaystyle\displaystyle 5\sum_{l=1}^{5}\frac{(-1)^{5+l}}{l}\sum_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{l}\geq 1\atop n_{1}+\ldots+n_{l}=5}\prod_{i=1}^{l}\frac{1}{{\Gamma(2n_{i}+2)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{93555}.

, one has

\gamma_{(-t^{4}y_{4})^{*}}=\gamma_{(t^{2}y_{2})^{*}}\gamma_{(-t^{2}y_{2})^{*}}
,
or equivalently,

\displaystyle\exp\Bigl(-\sum_{k\geq 1}\zeta(4k)\frac{t^{4k}}{k}\Bigr)=\frac{\sin(\mathrm{i}t\pi)}{\mathrm{i}t\pi}\frac{\sin(t\pi)}{t\pi}=\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{2(-4t\pi)^{4k}}{(4k+2)!}.

Since
\gamma_{(-t^{4}y_{4})^{*}}=\zeta((-t^{4}y_{4})^{*}),\gamma_{(-t^{2}y_{2})^{*}}=\zeta((-t^{2}y_{2})^{*}),\gamma_{(t^{2}y_{2})^{*}}=\zeta((t^{2}y_{2})^{*})

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}\zeta(\overbrace{{3,1,\ldots,3,1}}^{k{\tt\ times}})(-4t^{4})^{k}=\sum_{k\geq 0}\zeta(\overbrace{{4,\ldots,4}}^{k{\tt\ times}})(-t\pi)^{4k}=\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{2(-4t\pi)^{4k}}{(4k+2)!}.

\mathrm{span}_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\{\Sigma_{l}\}_{l\in\mathcal{L}ynY\setminus\{y_{1}\}}={\mathcal{R}}_{Y}\oplus\mathrm{span}_{{\mathbb{Q}}}{{\mathcal{L}}_{irr}^{\infty}(Y)})
.

LocaleCordonateIdentification(
N
)
%
N\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\geq 1}
%

{\mathcal{Z}}_{irr}^{\infty}({\mathcal{X}}):=\{\},{\mathcal{L}}_{irr}^{\infty}({\mathcal{X}}):=\{\},{\mathcal{R}}_{irr}({\mathcal{X}}):=\{\}
;

for
p
ranges in
2,\ldots,N
do %
{\mathcal{L}}yn^{p}({\mathcal{X}})
is the set of Lyndon words of weight
p
%

by elimination, obtain the system of equations in
\{\zeta(\Sigma_{l})\}_{l\in\mathcal{L}yn^{p}(X)}
;

by elimination, obtain the system of equations in
\{\zeta(S_{l})\}_{l\in\mathcal{L}yn^{p}(Y)}
;

for
l
ranges in the totally ordered
\mathcal{L}yn^{p}({\mathcal{X}})
do

identify the coefficient of
\Pi_{l}
in
Z_{\gamma}=B(y_{1})\pi_{Y}Z_{\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}}
;

identify the coefficient of
P_{l}
in
\pi_{X}Z_{\gamma}=B(x_{1})Z_{\mathop{{}_{{}^{\sqcup\!\sqcup}}}}

end_for;

for
l
ranges in the totally ordered
{\mathcal{L}}yn^{p}({\mathcal{X}})
do

express the local coordinate
\zeta(\Sigma_{l})
as rewriting rule;

if
\zeta(\Sigma_{l})\rightarrow\zeta(\Sigma_{l})

then
{\mathcal{Z}}_{irr}^{\infty}(Y)={\mathcal{Z}}_{irr}^{\infty}(Y)\cup\{\zeta(\Sigma_{l})\}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{irr}^{\infty}(Y)={\mathcal{L}}_{irr}^{\infty}(Y)\cup\{\Sigma_{l}\}

else
{\mathcal{R}}_{irr}(Y)={\mathcal{R}}_{irr}(Y)\cup\{\Sigma_{l}\rightarrow\Upsilon_{l}\}
;

express the local coordinate
\zeta(S_{l})
as rewriting rule;

if
\zeta(S_{l})\rightarrow\zeta(S_{l})

then
{\mathcal{Z}}_{irr}^{\infty}(X)={\mathcal{Z}}_{irr}^{\infty}(X)\cup\{\zeta(S_{l})\}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{irr}^{\infty}(X)={\mathcal{L}}_{irr}^{\infty}(X)\cup\{S_{l}\}

else
{\mathcal{R}}_{irr}(X)={\mathcal{R}}_{irr}(X)\cup\{S_{l}\rightarrow U_{l}\}

end_for

end_for

Proposition 3.30

(
[
40
,
41
]
)
.

\displaystyle{\mathbb{Q}}[\{S_{l}\}_{l\in\mathcal{L}ynX\setminus X}]={\mathcal{R}}_{X}\oplus{\mathbb{Q}}[{{\mathcal{L}}_{irr}^{\infty}(X)}],

\displaystyle{\mathbb{Q}}[\{\Sigma_{l}\}_{l\in\mathcal{L}ynY\setminus\{y_{1}\}}]={\mathcal{R}}_{Y}\oplus{\mathbb{Q}}[{\mathcal{L}}_{irr}^{\infty}(Y)].

Proof.

For any
w\in x_{0}X^{*}x_{1}
(resp.
(Y\setminus\{y_{1}\})Y^{*}
), using
{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}

and decomposing in the basis
\{S_{l}\}_{l\in\mathcal{L}ynX\setminus X}
(resp.
\{\Sigma_{l}\}_{l\in\mathcal{L}ynY\setminus\{y_{1}\}}
),

\zeta(w)\in{\mathbb{Q}}[{{\mathcal{Z}}_{irr}^{\infty}({\mathcal{X}})}]
.
Hence, for any
P\in{\mathbb{Q}}[\{S_{l}\}_{l\in\mathcal{L}ynX\setminus X}]
(resp.
{\mathbb{Q}}[\{\Sigma_{l}\}_{l\in\mathcal{L}ynY\setminus\{y_{1}\}}]
) such that

P\notin\ker\zeta\supseteq{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
, one obtains, by linearity,
\zeta(P)\in{\mathbb{Q}}[{\mathcal{Z}}_{irr}^{\infty}({\mathcal{X}})]
. Next, let

Q\in{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}\cap{\mathbb{Q}}[{\mathcal{L}}_{irr}^{\infty}({\mathcal{X}})]
. Since
{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}\subseteq\ker\zeta
then
\zeta(Q)=0
. Moreover,
restricted on
{\mathbb{Q}}[{\mathcal{L}}_{irr}^{\infty}({\mathcal{X}})]
, the polymorphism
\zeta
is bijective and then
Q=0
. It follows then the expected result.
∎

Example 3.31

(irreducible polyzetas up to weight
12
,
[

]
)
.

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Z}}_{irr}^{\leq 12}(X)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\zeta(S_{x_{0}x_{1}}),\zeta(S_{x_{0}^{2}x_{1}}),\zeta(S_{x_{0}^{4}x_{1}}),\zeta(S_{x_{0}^{6}x_{1}}),\zeta(S_{x_{0}x_{1}^{2}x_{0}x_{1}^{4}}),\zeta(S_{x_{0}^{8}x_{1}}),

\displaystyle\zeta(S_{x_{0}x_{1}^{2}x_{0}x_{1}^{6}}),\zeta(S_{x_{0}^{10}x_{1}}),\zeta(S_{x_{0}x_{1}^{3}x_{0}x_{1}^{7}}),\zeta(S_{x_{0}x_{1}^{2}x_{0}x_{1}^{8}}),\zeta(S_{x_{0}x_{1}^{4}x_{0}x_{1}^{6}})\}.

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{irr}^{\leq 12}(X)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{S_{x_{0}x_{1}},S_{x_{0}^{2}x_{1}},S_{x_{0}^{4}x_{1}},S_{x_{0}^{6}x_{1}},S_{x_{0}x_{1}^{2}x_{0}x_{1}^{4}},S_{x_{0}^{8}x_{1}},S_{x_{0}x_{1}^{2}x_{0}x_{1}^{6}},S_{x_{0}^{10}x_{1}},

\displaystyle S_{x_{0}x_{1}^{3}x_{0}x_{1}^{7}},S_{x_{0}x_{1}^{2}x_{0}x_{1}^{8}},S_{x_{0}x_{1}^{4}x_{0}x_{1}^{6}}\}.

\displaystyle\zeta(\Sigma_{y_{11}}),\zeta(\Sigma_{y_{2}y_{1}^{9}}),\zeta(\Sigma_{y_{3}y_{1}^{9}}),\zeta(\Sigma_{y_{2}^{2}y_{1}^{8}})\}.
